## AI Research

### [Drone WMDs Don’t Need Any New Technology](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bGoo3NWzMAzsLQceJ/drone-wmds-don-t-need-any-new-technology)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>This is a piece originally written for a national security audience at Frontiers. Although I think the ceiling of war is </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2kseP9fZghYHKLFno/superintelligence-vs-the-second-strike" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>much, much higher</span></i></a><i><span> than autopilot quadcopters, it's also important to understand how much AI is already lifting the floor, and just how </span></i><a href="https://michaelnotebook.com/vwh/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>vulnerable </span></i></a><i><span>the world is to accessible weapons of mass destruction. </span></i></p><hr /><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784571859/lexical_client_uploads/deshvjyqnstt4kiexzv7.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Drones are cheap, disposable, and the </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/mikebrown/2026/04/26/drones-are-the-biggest-military-revolution-in-a-century/"><span>future of war</span></a><span>. Over the past four years, we have seen platforms, missiles, and heavy infantry become increasingly obsolete in the face of $500 drones carrying a pack of explosives—a cost advantage that has let Iranians and Ukrainians alike </span><a href="https://www.cfr.org/articles/how-ukraines-drone-innovation-reversed-russias-momentum"><span>neuter the conventional capabilities</span></a><span> of their great power rivals. </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2025/03/03/world/europe/ukraine-russia-war-drones-deaths.html"><span>Eighty percent of casualties</span></a><span> in the </span><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/russias-grinding-war-ukraine"><span>bloodiest war since 1945</span></a><span> are from drone strikes, Russia has managed to lose </span><a href="https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/2025/september/russias-black-sea-failures-are-lessons-south-china-sea"><span>one-third of its fleet</span></a><span> to a country without a navy, and the US is </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/05/us/politics/rockets-iran-drones.html"><span>spending millions of dollars</span></a><span> to intercept five-figure Shaheds flying over the Strait of Hormuz.</span></p><p><span>All this is the result of a technology that is still immature. The violence inflicted by today’s drones is the handiwork of the scant few that manage to evade countermeasures (a mix of radio jamming, high-power microwave weapons, missiles, automatic cannons, interceptor drones, and nets) before making contact. These defenses exploit the inherent limitations of drones—human guidance, GPS feedback, flight exposure, radio links, range—to take them down en masse. And yet, even though </span><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/drone-saturation-russias-shahed-campaign"><span>75% of drones</span></a><span> manufactured today never reach their targets, they have nonetheless been strategically decisive in Ukraine and elsewhere.</span></p><p><span>These limitations will not hold for long. Just like bacteria being overexposed to antibiotics, overexposure to counterdrone tech has created an arms race for </span><a href="https://www.longwarjournal.org/archives/2025/06/ukrainian-intelligence-details-russias-new-v2u-autonomous-loitering-munition.php"><span>ever-more-autonomous</span></a><span> drone technologies. In the process of facilitating this arms race, states are likely to incrementally create and deploy an entirely new class of WMD—one that could provide rogue states with the nonnuclear means to threaten superpowers, or hand terrorists the means to selectively assassinate their political targets or civilians en masse.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, drone weapons intended for mass destruction have few barriers remaining to mass deployment. Even well before they reach the level of autonomy needed to surgically take out hardened targets on the battlefield, drones will be capable of employing their existing ability to navigate interiors, find and track human targets, and deploy simple antipersonnel devices to indiscriminately threaten civilians. Below, we discuss the looming arrival of miniature autonomous weapons, the limits of counterdrone technology, and the applications of drones as weapons of mass destruction.</span></p><h2><b><span>Breaking the Last Barriers to Autonomous Weapons</span></b></h2><p><span>The ideal drone weapon is a </span><a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/why-you-should-fear-slaughterbots-a-response"><span>slaughterbot</span></a><span>: a small, fully autonomous weapon system that can independently select and hunt its targets. For the most part, the necessary technology for such weapons already exists: airframes the size of a fist and the capability to track human targets are already on the front lines in the form of </span><a href="https://www.defenceukraine.com/en/insights/black-hornet-micro-uavs-ukraine-urban-combat/"><span>reconnaissance drones</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/how-russia-building-sovereign-drone-ecosystem-ai-driven-autonomy#:~:text=AI%2Denabled%20alternatives.-,Case%20Study%204%3A%20V2U%20and%20the%20Emergence%20of%20Fully%20Autonomous%20AI,innovative%20and%20dangerous%20unmanned%20systems%20currently%20observed%20in%20active%20combat%20use.,-Conclusion"><span>semiautonomous</span></a><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/how-russia-building-sovereign-drone-ecosystem-ai-driven-autonomy#:~:text=AI%2Denabled%20alternatives.-,Case%20Study%204%3A%20V2U%20and%20the%20Emergence%20of%20Fully%20Autonomous%20AI,innovative%20and%20dangerous%20unmanned%20systems%20currently%20observed%20in%20active%20combat%20use.,-Conclusion"><span> weapons</span></a><span> like the Russian V2U. Even now, these micro drones are agile and autonomous enough to hunt down and </span><a href="https://x.com/alextoussss/status/2077086243632873540"><span>kill</span></a><span> small moving targets like mosquitos—to say nothing of the drone technology advances expected in coming years.</span></p><p><span>From here, the only barrier to weaponization is integration: improving navigation enough to make drone technology useful for mass homicide in an urban setting, as well as packing the necessary guidance, sensor, and payload technology onto a small and energy-efficient chassis. Regrettably, this seems like less of an engineering problem than one of mission design: so long as the attacker is willing to accept indiscriminate targeting and use simple payloads aimed at civilians, the underlying technology is already—or very nearly—ready for practical use.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1ebe9ce758751257989184a54ecc82e4c0db7a55a5ffff9c46c3276857747317/ke4bflqlddybgpd6zwnr" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Interior navigation and mapping from an autonomous human-reconnaissance drone. Source: </span></i><a href="https://shield.ai/autonomy-for-the-world-indoor-exploration-with-nova-2/"><i><span>ShieldAI</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>To understand how close we are to these kinds of weapons, it helps to understand why we do not already employ fully autonomous drones. Right now, the </span><a href="https://static.rusi.org/tactical-developments-third-year-russo-ukrainian-war-february-2205.pdf"><span>most deadly</span></a><span> drones are small first-person-view (FPV) units, with the </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2025/03/03/world/europe/ukraine-russia-war-drones-deaths.html"><span>majority</span></a><span> of Russian and Ukrainian casualties alike stemming from direct drone strikes. For the most part, however, these small drones are being piloted directly by humans, either through a radio link or a spool of fiber-optic cable, with just the final leg of the attack being </span><a href="https://warroom.armywarcollege.edu/articles/ais-growing-role/"><span>delegated</span></a><span> to an AI targeting system.</span></p><p><span>So, </span><a href="https://nationalinterest.org/blog/buzz/ukraines-drones-can-now-kill-without-human-in-loop-sa-061226"><span>ethics aside</span></a><span>, why does drone warfare still depend on human pilots?</span></p><p><b><span>Distinguishing enemy targets from friendly assets on the battlefield still requires humans.</span></b><span> The main problem is that battlefields are intrinsically </span><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/ukraines-future-vision-and-current-capabilities-waging-ai-enabled-autonomous-warfare#h2-ai-in-automatic-target-recognition:~:text=Current%20Challenges%20in%20ATR"><span>adversarial environments</span></a><span>: an autonomous drone needs to avoid friendly fire on its own infrastructure and troops, anticipate pre-positioned counterdrone defenses, deal with camouflage and decoys, and destroy hardened targets like vehicles and infrastructure. This is especially complicated if you need drones to autonomously work together to accomplish an objective, such as by having specialized units target defenses to allow others through. For the moment, only humans have the skills to distinguish a camouflaged ally from an enemy unit, or to exploit the underbelly of an armored vehicle.</span></p><p><b><span>Designing drones for indiscriminate mass destruction is easier than for precise battlefield use. </span></b><span>Unfortunately, adversarial target selection is not a meaningful barrier for applying drones to mass terrorism. Destroying an armored vehicle needs the skill to reason about and single out its weak points; but, to kill an unarmored human, a drone need only make contact with them and deploy an explosive or poison needle. The targeting requirements and level of autonomy needed to indiscriminately massacre civilians, in other words, are much lower than what you’d need to selectively destroy hardened targets on a battlefield. In the words of Ukraine’s Azov brigade, “If you don’t care about civilians, you can simply </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/craigsmith/2026/03/26/fully-autonomous-drone-warfare-is-coming-to-ukraineand-iran/#:~:text=Azhnyuk%20argues%20that,hit%20like%20this.%E2%80%9D"><span>hit any target that moves</span></a><span>.”</span></p><p><b><span>Drones can already navigate indoor environments and track humans.</span></b><span> Aside from requiring guidance for target selection, autonomous drones also need the ability to navigate. Urban environments are cluttered and leave room for targets to shelter indoors, so drones must infiltrate and sweep through them to be maximally lethal. Autonomous navigation of this caliber already exists: </span><a href="https://shield.ai/autonomy-for-the-world-indoor-exploration-with-nova-2/"><span>as far back as 2022</span></a><span>, drones have been capable of mapping and tracking indoor environments to find humans, a skill used to </span><a href="https://www.politico.com/newsletters/national-security-daily/2023/12/21/rescuing-hostages-with-cheap-american-drones-00132750"><span>locate hostages</span></a><span> and scan through tunnels for enemy soldiers. These kinds of drones typically cost tens of thousands of dollars, but they have an expensive use case: infiltrating a GPS-denied location, then escaping to broadcast information. If you do not need the drone to survive and report back, and if you do not care if your drone can tell whether someone is surrendering or not, then you do not need </span><a href="https://globaldronehq.com/collections/thermal-drone-sensors"><span>expensive sensors</span></a><span> and plenty of onboard compute for decision-making—just the bare minimum to identify a target as human and fly at them.</span></p><p><b><span>Disposable drones that can autonomously attack civilians may soon be relatively cheap.</span></b><span> For comparison, a last-mile module, an upgrade chip that lets FPV drones </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2026/05/19/slaughterbots-now-ukraines-head-hunting-drones-terrify-russians/"><span>visually hunt down targets</span></a><span> when they lose connection, is about $500. One-way autonomous search, target selection, and mapping, at least for this anti-civilian use case, would likely be similarly inexpensive—already, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.13385"><span>visual</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.15305"><span>laser</span></a><span> mapping systems have been demonstrated, in principle, that can work their way around a room and track humans on a </span><a href="https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/autonomous-machines/embedded-systems/jetson-orin/nano-super-developer-kit/"><span>few hundred dollars</span></a><span> of hardware. If a military system that navigates to the entrance and then plans an indiscriminate suicide mission inside is not already achievable for just thousands of dollars, it will be in a </span><a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/autonomous-drone-warfare#:~:text=%E2%80%9CToday%2C%20we%20have,stations%2C%20and%20jammers."><span>matter of years</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Of course, most countries do not have the motivation to build these kinds of systems and drive down their unit economics. Discrimination, ethical or otherwise, is useful on the battlefield. If the costs of autonomous targeting keep falling or AI guidance improves, however, states might be tempted to start employing indiscriminate drones as a means of deterrence, or as a cheap way to enable terrorist proxies.</span></p><h2><b><span>Weaponization</span></b></h2><p><span>Given these capabilities, how could these drones be weaponized and delivered in practice? The main limiter on these kinds of small drones is energy: a modern 30g micro drone like a </span><a href="https://defense.flir.com/defense-products/black-hornet-3-prs/"><span>Black Hornet</span></a><span> can fly for about half an hour before needing to recharge, while a bigger FPV carrying an explosive payload will usually last only </span><a href="https://warontherocks.com/i-fought-in-ukraine-and-heres-why-fpv-drones-kind-of-suck/#:~:text=For%20sophisticated%20NATO,range%20of%20artillery."><span>15 minutes</span></a><span>. However, there are plenty of ways to stretch this energy budget further for the purpose of mass destruction, even without better battery technology.</span></p><p><b><span>Lethal payloads could be much smaller.</span></b><span>  Today, FPV units usually carry about a kilo of explosives, because they need to be </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/world/2025/jan/04/it-is-impossible-to-outrun-them-how-drones-transformed-war-in-ukraine"><span>flexible enough</span></a><span> to target vehicles and defensive infrastructure as well as enemy soldiers. If the goal is to break windows and kill humans, however, even just </span><a href="https://www.npaid.org/files/Mine-action-and-disarmarment/m85.pdf"><span>20g of frag explosives</span></a><span> is enough at several meters of distance, with much less needed for a lethal wound at near-contact. Alternatively, something as simple as a spring-loaded needle, coated with a microgram quantity of a poison like </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botulinum_toxin"><span>botulinum toxin</span></a><span> or a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novichok"><span>nerve agent</span></a><span>, would be immediately lethal on contact.</span></p><p><b><span>Energy expenditure on delivery and flight could be significantly reduced.</span></b><span> Rather than have the drones travel constantly under their own power, it is much more efficient to carry them into position with a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_carrier"><span>drone mothership</span></a><span> or even the </span><a href="https://www.twz.com/land/prsm-ballistic-missiles-loaded-with-coyote-drones-hatchet-mini-smart-bombs-eyed-by-army"><span>hull of a missile</span></a><span>. Once the drones are released into the air, they then need to navigate to a building without expending much power. A simple way to do this is to give the drone </span><a href="https://gwaramedia.com/en/ukraine-equips-fpv-drones-with-wings-increasing-their-flight-range-osint-analyst-says/"><span>glide wings</span></a><span>, letting it drift forward for most of its flight rather than loiter directly. This technique is </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2026/06/02/ukraines-new-fpvs-hit-targets-sixty-miles-behind-russian-lines/"><span>already used</span></a><span> in Ukraine to stretch the range of basic FPVs over 40 miles beyond the front line.</span></p><p><b><span>Drones could perch and idle, rather than hovering, while waiting for targets.</span></b><span> Finally, and most importantly, the drones would be ambush hunters. Rather than loiter in the air, it is much more efficient to </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2025/02/03/ukraines-ambush-drones-step-up-attacks-behind-enemy-lines"><span>perch and idle</span></a><span> under cover, running a milliwatt acoustic and visual sensor every few seconds until a target is detected. Although drones would still need to expend the energy to infiltrate a building, once inside, they could afford to act like improvised landmines </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2025/07/02/creeping-doom-russia-deploys-solar-powered-ambush-drones/"><span>for days or weeks</span></a><span> until their battery finally dies.</span></p><h2><b><span>Urban Drone Attack Sketch</span></b></h2><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ad8f3538cd2e301dd5c7b75ad94773d7ba4a24156bcbc71c260c1c16ae50bd7c/epf5rkrgulipu08zddbl" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Stylized micro drones tumbling out of a plane. Source: </span></i><a href="https://futureoflife.org/project/autonomous-weapons-systems/"><i><span>Future of Life Institute</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>To appreciate the implications of these capabilities, it helps to outline what a mass urban drone attack would actually look like.</span></p><p><span>At the outset, drones are delivered near the city through a large mothership, which independently stores them in a cargo hold. Depending on whether this is itself a larger drone, a plane, or the warhead of a missile, each could feasibly deliver anywhere from hundreds to tens of thousands of drones at once. Alternatively, the drones could be smuggled in through a pre-positioned </span><a href="https://www.defensenews.com/global/europe/2026/06/16/rheinmetall-pitches-shipping-container-that-can-spit-out-swarms-of-attack-drones/"><span>shipping container</span></a><span>, which then launches its contents from a nearby port or logistics yard. Either way, a large number of drones are then scattered above the city at a low altitude or are dispersed near street level.</span></p><p><span>How large? Assuming that the drones are arranged like capsuled quadcopters, they can be packed in extremely efficiently. Using the </span><a href="https://gaci.fr/build/front/pdf/Datasheet_NINOX-40.asset.pdf"><span>Ninox-40</span></a><span> system as an example, storage counts would be in the range of:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Delivery system</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hold volume</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Drone count</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Ballistic missile</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.5 m³</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~730</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Small plane</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>10 m³</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~14,000</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Box truck</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>33 m³</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~40,700</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Shipping container</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>67 m³</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>~82,700</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Once released, the drones glide down haphazardly, aiming to land in regions not already populated by other units. Some drift directly onto public crowds and attack their targets right away. Others do not land near a direct target, switching their focus to look for entrances, such as doorframes, windows, and tunnels. If the attacker is particularly sophisticated, these drones might be accompanied by a handful of larger drones carrying breaching charges, designed to create more openings for the main force. These drones then begin drifting through the building interior for a few minutes, looking for victims. If they cannot find a new target or are unable to find a route inside, they move to a dark corner or roadside and begin passively idling.</span></p><p><span>Many civilians would die in the initial attack. But the aftermath would be much worse. With the drones saturating the city, there would be no opportunity for any survivor to leave their barricade and seek help, and no way for resources and relief units to flow back in. Anyone in need of food or basic medical attention would be unreachable for weeks, during which the death toll would continue to mount. In effect, the entire city would be transformed into something akin to the </span><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3w2xqj9x13o"><span>Ukrainian front line</span></a><span> today, with the omnipresent threat of assassination forcing the surviving humans to </span><a href="https://www.thetimes.com/world/russia-ukraine-war/article/drones-front-line-soldiers-news-lj0vmh2ms"><span>slowly starve out</span></a><span> in isolated foxholes, without any chance of easy respite or rescue. But unlike on the front line, these humans would not be soldiers: most people will not have stashed food ahead of time, or armed themselves with anti-drone weapons, or sealed off every corner of their apartment with netting.</span></p><h2><b><span>Defense Against Autonomous Weapons</span></b></h2><p><span>Any improvements in drone technology will still need to contend with counterdrone defenses. It is precisely because drones are so threatening that states have </span><a href="https://www.nato.int/en/news-and-events/articles/news/2026/07/07/nato-allies-invest-40-billion-dollars-in-counter-drone-capabilities-and-drone-training"><span>invested heavily</span></a><span> in tools like </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus_Leonidas"><span>directed energy weapons</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/vikrammittal/2026/05/27/russias-yolka-interceptor-faces-challenges-against-ukrainian-drones/"><span>interceptor drones</span></a><span> to counter them. If drones become even more strategically dominant, then we will surely see correspondingly greater counterdrone efforts. So why should we expect this arms race to resolve in favor of the drones, rather than their countermeasures?</span></p><p><b><span>Future drones will be less vulnerable to radio-frequency jamming.</span></b><span> To appreciate the limitations of counterdrone technology, we can look at the difficulty states are already experiencing in their efforts to counter </span><a href="https://united24media.com/war-in-ukraine/can-fiber-optic-drones-be-stopped-how-ukraine-faces-the-unjammable-threat-12502"><span>fiber-optic FPV units</span></a><span>. These drones work by unspooling a thin fiber-optic cable behind them, letting a human pilot them directly for up to </span><a href="https://en.defence-ua.com/industries/ukrainians_made_an_fpv_with_fiber_optic_cord_stretching_for_41_km-13327.html"><span>40 kilometers</span></a><span> without having to worry about GPS or input jamming.</span></p><p><span>The reason these drones are so effective is that they’re </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/world/europe/ukraine-russia-drones-fiber-optic-cable-6c96a9f1"><span>naturally resilient</span></a><span> against common counterdrone techniques. Throughout the Ukraine war, the </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2024/03/12/world/europe/ukraine-drone-russia-jamming.html"><span>most important</span></a><span> anti-drone tool has been radio-frequency jamming. As long as a human pilot is selecting targets and telling the drone where to go, or as long as the drone depends on GPS coordinates to navigate, overwhelming or spoofing those broadcasts with a countersignal will cause the drones to fly harmlessly off course. With a fiber-optic drone, all the piloting happens through a direct data link, so this kind of countersignal is harmless. The same is true of any autonomous drone—as long as all the decision making is processed on board, there’s </span><a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/autonomous-drone-warfare#:~:text=%E2%80%9CI%20think%20in,much%20larger%20scale."><span>no human input to jam or spoof</span></a><span> in the first place.</span></p><p><b><span>Interception is asymmetrically difficult against small, stealthy drones. </span></b><span>In cases where it’s difficult to achieve an electronic soft kill on the drone, the backup option is to physically destroy them with a kinetic interceptor. The reason this is a backup is that it’s expensive and prone to blind spots. Small FPVs are cheap and agile enough that it’s easy to </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2025/09/10/why-some-anti-drone-artillery-comes-at-a-sky-high-price/"><span>spend much more</span></a><span> to down them than they’re worth, especially when relying on the expensive autocannon </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2024/11/06/spiking-the-guns-precision-drone-strikes-destroy-russian-artillery/"><span>in the path of a flying explosive</span></a><span>. This is why direct kinetic interception is </span><a href="https://www.aspistrategist.org.au/the-challenge-of-cheap-drones-finding-an-even-cheaper-way-to-destroy-them/"><span>usually reserved for expensive drones</span></a><span> with fixed flight paths (like Shaheds) and kept as an option of </span><a href="https://frontliner.ua/en/a-last-resort-shot-how-ukrainian-innovation-takes-down-enemy-drones/"><span>last resort</span></a><span> for small drones.</span></p><p><span>There is also the problem of terrain blindness: if you cannot see a drone, you cannot shoot it down. </span><a href="https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/AD1152139.pdf"><span>Modern LSS</span></a><span> (low, small, slow) drones are already so tiny that they are difficult for radar to distinguish from birds, trees, and ground clutter, allowing operators to fly them near the treeline until they are too close to reliably intercept. This is especially problematic in an urban environment, where there are many places to hide and many opportunities for collateral damage.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/b80ed6f90f109b9aacbfee83cef7195dbdfd53ed1d67257bfaf16242da550c6d/bkjfgl5qcet2sp5t0rs5" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Anti-drone nets in Druzhkivka, Ukraine. Source: </span></i><a href="https://www.reuters.com/pictures/diary-druzhkivka-inside-frontline-ukrainian-town-under-constant-fire-2026-06-22/"><i><span>Reuters/Nina Liashonok</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Physical barriers and EMP weapons will struggle to reach sufficient coverage in cities. </span></b><span>Instead, the most effective tools against autonomous drones will be structural barriers and directed energy weapons. One of the most visible effects of the war on Ukrainian infrastructure is the miles-long corridor of nets that cover </span><a href="https://www.npr.org/2026/03/17/nx-s1-5743446/russia-ukraine-war-nets-drones"><span>roads</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2026/05/05/drone-hide-and-seek-fpvs-are-changing-the-rules-of-urban-warfare/"><span>even cities</span></a><span>, without which transports would be constantly exposed to drone strikes. But nets are not hard barriers: if the initial carrier punches through, if a few specialized units carry </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidhambling/2025/02/17/ukrainian-drone-pilots-unimpressed-by-russias-anti-fpv-tunnel/"><span>breaching tools</span></a><span>, or if the drones are released from below, there is little to stop them from flying unimpeded and sieging the streets beneath. To actually destroy the drones efficiently, the best candidate is a directed energy weapon, particularly an </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epirus_Leonidas"><span>electromagnetic pulse</span></a><span> (EMP) device. It creates an electric field powerful enough to short-circuit any electronics in range, physically destroying the drone controllers and motors. Unfortunately, cities are far from ideal places to deploy such weapons; building material is reasonably effective at </span><a href="https://ece-research.unm.edu/summa/notes/In/IN624.pdf"><span>shielding</span></a><span> against an electric pulse, which means that, if the EMP does not catch the drones in the initial sortie, lack of a clean sight line will significantly weaken this countermeasure against drones dispersed throughout a city.</span></p><p><span>In other words, these defenses can be locally effective but struggle to get sufficient coverage, especially over an </span><a href="https://bylinetimes.com/2026/01/13/how-kherson-became-a-live-testing-ground-for-drone-defence-against-russias-human-safari-of-ukrainians/"><span>active residential area</span></a><span>. They are also, of course, proactive defenses: they have to be installed ahead of time in order to have any defensive effect, which gives the attacker time to assess and plan around them.</span></p><h2><b><span>Strategic Applications for Rogue Actors</span></b></h2><p><span>In summary, small lethal autonomous weapons will be very difficult to defend against, especially in the context of securing large urban environments. This leaves the question of which actors would want to use them, and to what end?</span></p><p><b><span>Advanced drones would be more useful for rogue states than for superpowers. </span></b><span>The first countries to acquire fully autonomous drones will be those that have a precision manufacturing base and frontier AIs capable of helping with military R&amp;D: namely, the US and China. However, autonomous drones are only marginally useful for the existing great powers: a new option to </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/china/taiwan-needs-hornets-nest-drones-deter-conflict-us-diplomat-says-2026-07-02/"><span>further deter</span></a><span> a Taiwanese invasion, a cheaper way to conduct </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Qasem_Soleimani"><span>remote assassinations</span></a><span>, and a way to maintain conventional parity with rivals’ own drone mass. Rather than meaningfully changing the balance of power between nuclear states, near-term autonomous drones will likely be most useful for rogue actors seeking new weapons of mass destruction to enhance deterrence. In this regard, indiscriminate autonomous drone swarms possess many advantages.</span></p><p><b><span>Drones are inherently simple, which makes it very difficult to control their proliferation.</span></b><span> A central reason why modern military drones are so cheap and widespread is their simple design, combined with the </span><a href="https://foreignpolicy.com/2013/04/29/epiphanies-from-chris-anderson/"><span>commodification</span></a><span> of key components like memory and compute. As we have seen with </span><a href="https://www.aei.org/research-products/report/the-impact-of-semiconductor-sanctions-on-russia/"><span>semiconductor sanctions</span></a><span> on Russia, the underlying materials are too accessible in </span><a href="https://thebulletin.org/2021/04/meet-the-future-weapon-of-mass-destruction-the-drone-swarm/#:~:text=In%20October%202016,are%20absolutely%20possible.)"><span>ordinary consumer supply chains</span></a><span> to easily deny mass production. Moreover, even if a specific country could be cordoned off from general drone production, Russia and China have </span><a href="https://www.spf.org/iina/en/articles/lee_07.html#:~:text=It%20has%20long,and%20strike%20roles."><span>proved willing</span></a><span> to help export their military designs to allies. Even terrorist groups might be able to build, or at least acquire, large quantities of drones for urban attacks, either through covert smuggling or </span><a href="https://www.cfr.org/articles/irans-support-houthis-what-know#:~:text=Iran%20is%20the,Studies%20in%202023."><span>state sponsorship</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Drones can be used conventionally, not just for WMDs, making restrictions hard to enforce. </span></b><span>To the extent that states can impose restrictions on drone manufacturing or acquisition, they have largely failed to do so. This is partly because there is not a clear point of intervention in drone development: unlike other WMDs, whose primary purposes are for terror and leverage, any state building drones can claim that they are for civilian or conventional military use rather than for mass destruction. As a result, international organizations like the UN have done little more than to condemn the principle of autonomous weapons, without yet addressing basic questions like the </span><a href="https://news.un.org/en/story/2025/05/1163256"><span>definition of autonomy</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Drone swarms are a far more precise, controllable deterrent than other nonnuclear WMDs.</span></b><span> Chemical weapons, although useful for terror, are difficult to widely disperse and threaten entire cities with. This combination of extreme fear and limited destruction thus invites the risk of </span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/jpr/advance-article/doi/10.1093/jopres/xjag001/8524883"><span>extreme escalation</span></a><span> in retaliation, making them poor deterrents. On the opposite end of the spectrum, bioweapons are simply </span><a href="https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/01495930802358364"><span>too destructive</span></a><span>, symmetrically threatening those who deploy them, as well as too invisible and delayed to create an immediate effect. Massive drone swarms, however, could be used to reliably siege an entire city while remaining contained within it.</span></p><p><b><span>Autonomous weapons are fundamentally hard to stop, particularly when aimed at civilians.</span></b><span> Even if states are able to secure </span><a href="https://breakingdefense.com/2025/02/high-power-microwave-force-field-knocks-drone-swarms-from-sky/#:~:text=The%20CONOPs%20would,six%20of%20these."><span>individual targets</span></a><span>, such as military bases and political offices, securing the whole of society such that there are no soft targets for advanced drones would be enormously challenging. Even aside from their sheer size, cities are </span><a href="https://bylinetimes.com/2026/01/13/how-kherson-became-a-live-testing-ground-for-drone-defence-against-russias-human-safari-of-ukrainians/"><span>difficult to cover</span></a><span> because they are both open enough to let people move around freely and dense enough that interceptors will usually lack a clear line of sight, risking collateral damage. If this technology were to proliferate widely, the future might be one of constant and extreme geopolitical tension, where even minor military powers are constantly tempted to assemble large swarms of murderous drones as deterrents.</span></p><h2><b><span>Taking Future Technology Seriously</span></b></h2><p><span>This piece is an exercise in taking the future of military technology seriously. That means being credulous that new offensive technologies are possible and imagining what a serious effort to abuse and counter them would look like in practice. It means asking ourselves what the logistics of putting nets and energy platforms over New York would be. It means looking at what it is like to live in a trench on the </span><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c23gjk7dlvlo" rel="noreferrer"><span>Ukrainian frontline</span></a><span>, and asking if these kinds of experiences will always stay so far away.</span></p><p><span>Taking future technology seriously also means looking further ahead. By all appearances, the kind of indiscriminate weapon described above is not far off. Autonomous drones that blindly hunt down humans and siege cities, if they do not already exist, are held back more by ethics and military opportunity cost than any fundamental engineering problems. But there’s no reason to imagine that dumb, flying landmines are as far as drone technology will progress. Drones the </span><a href="https://www.scmp.com/news/china/science/article/3315206/chinese-military-robotics-lab-creates-mosquito-sized-microdrone-covert-operations"><span>size of mosquitoes</span></a><span>, drones as cheap as bullets, drones so numerous their swarms </span><a href="https://funker530.com/video/us-drone-swarm-tech-blocks-out-the-sun"><span>blot out the sky</span></a><span>, drones that </span><a href="https://aerial-core.eu/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/applsci-13-10175-v2.pdf"><span>sit on your power lines</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.google.com/search?q=solar+ambush+drone+russia&amp;oq=solar+ambush+drone+russia&amp;gs_lcrp=EgZjaHJvbWUyBggAEEUYOTIGCAEQRRg8MgYIAhBFGDwyBggDEEUYPdIBCDk3MjJqMGo0qAIAsAIA&amp;sourceid=chrome&amp;source=chrome.ob&amp;ie=UTF-8"><span>in the sun</span></a><span> and never leave. These are not only possible but inevitable: if all that happens is just the normal grinding of drone engineering and mass production, cheap swarms of thousands, or even millions, of killer drones will eventually be universally available, in the same way that trench warfare was inevitable the day the British fielded the maxim gun for the first time. Whether this happens in 5, 10, or 15 years is much less important than whether we are </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GOVPUB-D301-PURL-gpo139494/pdf/GOVPUB-D301-PURL-gpo139494.pdf"><span>prepared to deal with it</span></a><span> when it does. Some policies, like </span><a href="https://www.orfonline.org/research/a-plague-on-the-horizon-concerns-on-the-proliferation-of-drone-swarms#:~:text=for%20military%20purposes.-,Combatting%20Proliferation,-States%20concerned%20about"><span>nonproliferation and defensive investment</span></a><span>, can only work while the threat is still unrealized. Implementing these policies for drones, and any other future military, starts with taking what will be possible tomorrow seriously and planning for it today.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bGoo3NWzMAzsLQceJ/drone-wmds-don-t-need-any-new-technology#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Stop doing decision theory without metaphysics](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oZzRHiSZPcjrWHeoE/stop-doing-decision-theory-without-metaphysics)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>[Epistemic status: rant]</span></i></p><p><span>There’s something that annoys me about the reoccurring debates on decision theory in this corner of the internet.</span></p><p><span>Take a simple blackmail scenario:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Omega, a near-perfect predictor, knows a piece of embarrassing information about you. He threatens you that he will release it to the public if you don’t pay him 100$. However, making the threat is slightly costly to him, and he wouldn’t have done it if he hadn’t predicted that you would pay. Do you pay?</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Let’s say we want to argue for the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060"><span>Functional Decision Theory</span></a><span> (FDT) answer that you shouldn’t pay. It was originally motivated by the observation that such agents seem to achieve higher utility (“</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4ARtkT3EYox3THYjF/rationality-is-systematized-winning"><span>rationality is about winning</span></a><span>”), since they don’t get blackmailed in the first place. But that only leads to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w9vQNTSm7dthgathH/reframing-lesswrong-style-decision-theory-as-commitment" rel="noreferrer"><span>making yourself</span></a><span> into </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/son-of-cdt"><span>such an agent </span></a><span>in advance (which everybody generally agrees you should do</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzghtg3edk1q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzghtg3edk1q">[1]</a></sup></span><span>) - it doesn’t clearly apply when you are already being blackmailed and have never thought about the question before, or if you are an AI who was just created and is instantly blackmailed before being able to self-modify or make precommitments.</span></p><p><span>I see three broad ways to make FDT’s recommendation make sense in that case</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefakns948yz85"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnakns948yz85">[2]</a></sup></span><span>:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Alternate realities: You are in a branch of reality where you got blackmailed, but other branches exist, and you can make it so you don’t get blackmailed there. For example, this is how people usually talk about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/updateless-decision-theory"><span>Updateless Decision Theory (UDT)</span></a><span>, and Paul Christiano seems to understand it like this</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk8rot2gi0u8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk8rot2gi0u8">[3]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Changing the past / making this reality impossible: There is only one reality, but deciding not to pay retroactively makes it so you didn’t get blackmailed in the first place / makes the reality where you get blackmailed impossible, and makes it so something else actually happens. For example, Joe Carlsmith </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2021/08/27/can-you-control-the-past/"><span>here</span></a><span> at least explores this direction, and there’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/timeless-decision-theory"><span>Timeless Decision Theory (TDT)</span></a><span>-style framings like Nate Soares </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RhAxxPXrkcEaNArnd/notes-on-can-you-control-the-past#Parfit_s_hitchhiker_and_contradicting_the_problem_statement"><span>here</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/04/07/decisions-are-for-making-bad-outcomes-inconsistent/"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Platonism: You might be in the abstract object of the computation that is your decision process, which determines whether you get blackmailed in actual reality. For example, this is arguably the most natural way to think about the FDT paper’s causal graphs, where the node with your decision function is upstream of the physical world.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx0zoco36xjd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx0zoco36xjd">[4]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>These are all metaphysical claims - therefore, you need metaphysics to make FDT make sense.</span></p><p><span>(I understand many people will disagree with me on this - I think it’s a widespread misconception. I address some counterarguments in this footnote</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefonz65qe8r9a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnonz65qe8r9a">[5]</a></sup></span><span>)</span></p><p><span>And I want to be absolutely clear here - I am very sympathetic to these claims! In my personal opinion, something in the vicinity is actually true. But… they </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> metaphysical claims, and ones that most people probably don’t immediately accept.</span></p><p><span>So let’s say that someone only knows this fact about the topic, and and starts reading what people are saying about it online. Imagine their surprise at finding that the back-and-forths between FDTers and anti-FDTers are </span><i><span>largely not about metaphysics</span></i><span>. Huh?</span></p><p><span>What the hell is going on?</span></p><p><span>I understand that metaphysics is really annoying and hard to think about, but… guys, I think this is your actual crux!</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>Take </span><a href="https://benthams.substack.com/p/functional-decision-theory-not-even/comments"><span>the comment section under Bentham’s Bulldog’s recent post about FDT</span></a><span>. There are such luminaries as Scott Alexander and Stuart Armstrong chiming in. Yet barely anyone is bringing up metaphysics - instead the discussions circle around semantic disagreements about what the words “rational” and “decision theory” should mean, confusion that FDT is necessary for everyday psychological precommitments</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflh2whudcmfa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlh2whudcmfa">[6]</a></sup></span><span>, confusion that everyday psychological precommitments are sufficient to become an FDT agent</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffjuy9yy1d1b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfjuy9yy1d1b">[7]</a></sup></span><span>, confusion that the observation that FDT agents get more utility is enough to fully justify FDT</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreft4l7sughof"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnt4l7sughof">[8]</a></sup></span><span>, and so on and so forth.</span></p><p><span>No! What are you doing? You have one very concrete disagreement - talk about that one! There is only one spot where FDT and a normal worldview diverge, and it’s the kind of scenario above, where you have no time to self-modify/precommit. For everything else, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w9vQNTSm7dthgathH/reframing-lesswrong-style-decision-theory-as-commitment" rel="noreferrer"><span>normal decision theory is sufficient</span></a><span>!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5ir50s1lgto"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5ir50s1lgto">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>And if you talk about that kind of scenario, you’ll be able to actually get to the bottom of your disagreements - which is metaphysics.</span></p><p><span>Concretely, my message to FDT proponents is this: </span><b><span>Start being clear to yourself, and to others, about your metaphysical stances. You are confusing everyone by leaving them implicit.</span></b></p><p><span>I have a suspicion that a substantial amount of the resistance to FDT is that people can tell that you’re doing something fucky. They can tell that FDT doesn’t </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> make sense without additional metaphysical commitments, and that you’re just pretending it does. But if you were to say “you might just be inside the abstract computation” or whatever, I think people would agree that FDT makes sense </span><i><span>given that assumption</span></i><span>. And then you can actually talk about whether those metaphysics are reasonable and how to handle that, instead of talking past each other.</span></p><p><span>Old-school LessWrong was more explicit about this stuff. There was plenty of open talk, back when UDT was the flagship decision theory, about crazy stuff like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NvwJMQvfu9hbBdG6d/preferences-without-existence"><span>everything existing</span></a><span>, that we can </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RcvyJjPQwimAeapNg/torture-vs-dust-vs-the-presumptuous-philosopher-anthropic"><span>decide what gets to “exist”</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jZvTWB6Z75cv2q3Nw/i-like-simplicity-but-not-that-much"><span>supernatural voices from the sky</span></a><span>, and so on. My guess is that a lot of those veterans from back then are aware of what I’m saying here, and have just gotten tired of talking about metaphysics and don’t chime in much anymore.</span></p><p><span>For example, Paul Christiano:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7nAxgQYGYrEY5ZCAD/l-zombies-l-zombies?commentId=A5tLGnypzkSpQsJ8y"><span>“What the physical world actually looks like” seems likely to be a matter of preferences… probably I’d go with one of “avoid thinking about it“ or “move away from ascribing metaphysical significance to existing,” or at least moving towards a state of ignorance.</span></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span>(on his take on decision theory) </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9W4TQvixiQjpZmzrx/decision-theory-and-dynamic-inconsistency"><span>I justify that perspective in significant part from a position of radical uncertainty: I’m not sure if I’m thinking about worlds that don’t exist, or if it’s </span><i><span>us</span></i><span> who don’t exist and there is some real world somewhere thinking about us.</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span>Paul Christiano is not the type of writer to say something that wild if it’s not necessary for what he’s arguing for! It’s really not as simple as some of you think!</span></p><p><span>I think something has gotten lost somewhere, in the transition to the more narrow, academic framing of FDT. Some of the newer arrivals to the space didn’t get the message that there is an underlying metaphysical question, and are going around thinking that it’s a normal decision theory like any other. My past self from three years ago definitely didn’t fully realize what it was arguing for when it was arguing for FDT.</span></p><p><span>I mean, let’s just take a look at </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FBbHEjkZzdupcjkna/miri-op-exchange-about-decision-theory-1"><span>these apparent facts about the paper</span></a><span> (emphasis mine):</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span>Scott Garrabrant: </span></i><span>I think that there is this (obvious to LessWrongers, because it is deeply entangled with the entire LessWrong philosophy) ontology in which </span><b><span>“I am an algorithm” rather than “I am a physical object.”</span></b><span> I think that most decision theorists haven’t really considered this ontology. I mostly view FDT (the paper) as a not-fully-formal attempt to bridge that inferential difference and argue for identifying with your algorithm… the heart of FDT is about the algorithm question.</span></p><p><span>…</span></p><p><i><span>Rob Bensinger: </span></i><span>Agreed the FDT paper was mainly about the algorithm axis.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The FDT paper is close to my heart and I respect the authors a lot, but… if this was the intention, why are you writing a </span><i><span>pure decision theory</span></i><span> paper? That’s a metaphysical claim. And there is no mention of metaphysical commitments in the paper at all - in fact, in the conclusion, it explicitly claims that it all works without any metaphysics (which just isn’t true, in my opinion - maybe on a formal level, but not on a philosophical level). That seems almost deceptive - no wonder that this confuses everyone, including the referees of the paper.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wXbSAKu2AcohaK2Gt/udt-shows-that-decision-theory-is-more-puzzling-than-ever"><span>Wei Dai</span></a><span> (not claiming that he would fully agree with my take here):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I feel like MIRI perhaps mispositioned FDT (their variant of UDT) as a clear advancement in decision theory, whereas maybe they could have attracted more attention/interest from academic philosophy if the framing was instead that the UDT line of thinking shows that decision theory is just more deeply puzzling than anyone had previously realized.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Yeah - I could imagine another version of the paper, something like “computationalism applied to decision theory leads to weird metaphysical challenges like seemingly changing the past and difficult formal problems like logical counterfactuals if we take it seriously. But we should - because it’s very elegant and solves a lot of issues (e.g. dynamic inconsistency, coordination with similar agents), and computationalism is a widespread position.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgu8sypw3n9a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngu8sypw3n9a">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>At this point, I see the failure of academia to come up with FDT before LessWrong partially as the same academic failure mode, of too little </span><i><span>interdisciplinarity,</span></i><span> that caused the FDT paper authors to feel pressure to stay unnaturally agnostic about metaphysics. In retrospect, it’s obvious that the concepts of logical causation and updatelessness have been grasped at in academia for a long time</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo2t3jb6lyii"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno2t3jb6lyii">[11]</a></sup></span><span>, but that they just never fully got there - because it needs metaphysics to actually make sense. But decision theory and metaphysics are different subfields of philosophy, so bringing them together doesn’t come naturally. Grand unifying theories are institutionally difficult, and often actively disincentivized. LessWrongers, on the other hand, were not academics, and nonconformist enough to put together the obvious pieces lying around</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkcru4q3ydg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkcru4q3ydg">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>But then, let’s be clear about what we’re actually doing! Let’s be proud of the metaphysics again! Let’s be proud of being generalist interdisciplinary thinkers!</span></p><p><span>Getting to this point via the heuristic of “rationality is about winning” was perfectly valid. We did a great job! We outdid the academics! But that just means we get to argue about metaphysics now</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbze4thpwu79"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbze4thpwu79">[13]</a></sup></span><span>. We leveled up.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>Finally, I’d like to end on a positive note - Nate Soares in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RhAxxPXrkcEaNArnd/notes-on-can-you-control-the-past"><span>Notes on “Can you control the past”</span></a><span> is who I’ve seen make things explicit the most in the last few years (although still not as much as I would like). For example, he says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I agree “you’re flat-out metaphysically wrong (in a way that seems even worse than violating [the principle of not passing up] guaranteed payoffs)” is a valid counterargument to my actual position (in a way that “you violate [the principle of not passing up] guaranteed payoffs” is not). :-)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Then Joe Carlsmith doesn’t press him any further on this, presumably in part, again, because metaphysics seems too annoying to get into. But… yes! More of this please!</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Hein de Haan and @eigengender for valuable discussion of these ideas.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzghtg3edk1q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzghtg3edk1q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>even by academic decision theorists - although they might not fully realize the implications, e.g. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w9vQNTSm7dthgathH/reframing-lesswrong-style-decision-theory-as-commitment" rel="noreferrer"><span>that you need to become the kind of person who would choose to burn to death in Bomb*</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnakns948yz85"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefakns948yz85">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Assuming the predictor isn’t predicting you by simulating you with high fidelity (since otherwise you can just say that you might be in the simulation). This is reasonable to postulate because FDT is also supposed to change your action with very bad prediction on the part of Omega, e.g. with only a 60% success rate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk8rot2gi0u8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk8rot2gi0u8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9W4TQvixiQjpZmzrx/decision-theory-and-dynamic-inconsistency" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>“I’m not sure if I’m thinking about worlds that don’t exist, or if it’s </span><i><span>us</span></i><span> who don’t exist and there is some real world somewhere thinking about us.”</span></a></p><p><span>Note that it’s also technically possible to justify this in a purely axiological way, that you just also </span><i><span>care about </span></i><span>alternate realities, without making a metaphysical claim as to whether they’re real. But I don’t buy this, this is just a formal trick - you wouldn’t care about something that you don’t, on a gut level, believe is “real” in some way.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx0zoco36xjd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx0zoco36xjd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s a little ambiguous, but from the paper: “What’s remarkable about this line of reasoning is that even in the case where Fiona has observed that box B is full, when she envisions two-boxing, she envisions a scenario where she instead (with high probability) sees that the box is empty. In words, she reasons: “The thoughts I’m currently thinking are the decision procedure that I run upon seeing a full box. This procedure is being predicted by the predictor, and (maybe) implemented by my body. If it outputs onebox, the box is likely full and my brain implements this procedure so I take one box. If instead it outputs twobox, the box is likely empty and my brain does not implement this procedure (because I will be shown an empty box). Thus, if this procedure outputs onebox then I’m likely to keep $1,000,000; whereas if it outputs twobox I’m likely to get only $1,000. Outputting onebox leads to better outcomes, so this decision procedure hereby outputs onebox.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnonz65qe8r9a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefonz65qe8r9a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>(This footnote is very long, so probably actually click on it instead of hovering over it). Three out of three non-experts that I spoke to about this had this misconception. It makes me think that the metaphysics-agnostic framing in the FDT paper might genuinely have had bad effects here. Anyway, here are some counterarguments and alternative framings I’ve encountered, and my responses:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>More on impossible realities: “But standard academic decision theories need to consider counterpossibles too, since every counterfactual presupposes something that isn’t true (that we might do something other than what we actually will). So there’s no more metaphysics than in a normal worldview”. Okay, sure, but they don’t imagine that they can make their </span><i><span>own</span></i><span> reality impossible - only FDT does that (academic decision theories only imagine inconsistencies insofar as they can actually make them consistent after all, by choosing that option). That, to me, is a distinct stance - a much more metaphysical one. It doesn’t make sense in a normal worldview.</span><br /><br /><span>Then the response is often “but the impossible realities never actually happen, it’s purely concentrated in counterfactuals”. Well, sure, but the consideration is always present in this framing. If Omega is imperfect, you’re making decisions by imagining an x% chance that you’re making this reality impossible with your action (and that something else actually happened). What you’re really saying (like Nate Soares </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RhAxxPXrkcEaNArnd/notes-on-can-you-control-the-past"><span>here</span></a><span>, ctrl-F “metaphysically”) is “You need to consider crazy metaphysics (imagining you’re in a hypothetical impossible reality) to make decisions in a reasonable way”, and like… yes, exactly. That’s </span><i><span>de facto</span></i><span> a metaphysical stance. “It makes things easier if we imagine this” is not a decisive argument for a metaphysical claim that permits you to leave it unstated as if it’s obvious. (This line of argument sometimes feels like people are really trying their absolute hardest to pretend they’re not taking unusual metaphysical stances, even though they are.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Pseudo-simulation (this is the other way I see to interpret the causal graph framing in the FDT paper): “Even if Omega isn’t predicting you by simulating you with high fidelity, if your decision function is simple and doesn’t take your full sense experience into account, the computation that you </span><i><span>actually are</span></i><span> (your decision function) would by definition still exist in Omega’s head, even if your full self doesn’t exist - so there’s no paradox since the computation that you actually are (your decision function) is still instantiated somewhere.” Nate Soares frames it like this </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RhAxxPXrkcEaNArnd/notes-on-can-you-control-the-past#Who_am_I_"><span>here</span></a><span>, and I’ve encountered it in personal conversation. This is a useful framing in general, but I don’t think it ends up working for this.</span><br /><br /><span>If we assume I’m actually being blackmailed (like the problem statement says), I have access to my full sense experience. So naively, I have very high credence in the belief that my sense experience exists / is actually instantiated somewhere. But the reasoning above is predicated on thinking that it might not be. In fact, to make the math work out the same way, you have to be </span><i><span>completely</span></i><span> </span><i><span>agnostic</span></i><span> about whether your sense experience is happening, as if you have no information about it whatsoever (since even a little bit of updating on it would bias you towards paying more than FDT recommends). To be clear, not agnostic about whether your sense experience is accurate about the external world - agnostic about </span><i><span>whether it’s happening at all. </span></i><span>That’s not </span><i><span>technically</span></i><span> metaphysical, I suppose, but it’s a very radical epistemological stance that most people would still disagree with. (it rejects Descartes!) Also, it’s just obviously wrong, and you don’t actually believe this. The people I’ve talked to have all also denied that this kind of radical skepticism is needed to justify FDT - I’m including it here more for completeness.</span><br /><br /><span>(Note that I am </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> saying here that you can fool Omega by taking your full sense experience into account in your decision - I’m purely analyzing what this reasoning actually looks like, and whether it makes sense as stated, or whether there is an additional claim needed to reach the correct answer that you shouldn’t pay.)</span><br /><br /><span>Again, then you might say “but this never actually happens”, and, no, even when Omega is imperfect, in this framing you have an x% credence that your sense experience isn’t actually happening.</span></li></ul><p><span>A few other, less common counterarguments:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>“By posing this problem, you are already assuming that you are in a situation that is vanishingly unlikely by FDT’s lights - so it’s not really a good counterargument that it performs badly there by not paying.”: I think that this is a good intuition aid, but it’s not really an explanation that would make sense to the person already being blackmailed. It doesn’t clearly matter to them that the situation they are in is “unlikely” in some abstract sense. For that, you need one of the metaphysical claims.</span></li><li value="2"><span>“We need to deflate/dissolve the notions of existence and instantiation.” My personally preferred solution, but also clearly a metaphysical move.</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlh2whudcmfa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflh2whudcmfa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>No - you can decide to e.g. become a virtuous, reliable agent on entirely causal reasoning.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfjuy9yy1d1b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffjuy9yy1d1b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>No - it only leads to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/son-of-cdt"><span>son-of-CDT</span></a><span> (which, to be fair, is pretty powerful, as I discuss </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w9vQNTSm7dthgathH/reframing-lesswrong-style-decision-theory-as-commitment" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, but fails on the blackmail scenario above).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnt4l7sughof"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreft4l7sughof">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s not, as we see in the blackmail scenario above.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ir50s1lgto"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ir50s1lgto">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note for example, that XOR Blackmail - in the way it’s usually understood, which is generally taken to disprove EDT - is such a problem. So it’s really more of a counterexample to updatefulness, than to EDT-style counterfactuals.</span></p><p><span>I am generally using FDT to mean updatelessness here - sorry for being imprecise (although FDT is supposed to be “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FBbHEjkZzdupcjkna/miri-op-exchange-about-decision-theory-1"><span>an umbrella-term for UDT-ish approaches to decision theory</span></a><span>”, so it’s pretty close. It was originally meant to be agnostic between EDT- and CDT-style counterfactuals, so I usually take the disagreement about those to not be central. And it probably doesn’t matter anyway, since “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/fgHSwxFitysGKHH56"><span>CDT=EDT?</span></a><span>”.and updatelessness is the more important proposal, in my view.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngu8sypw3n9a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgu8sypw3n9a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>(Joe Carlsmith hits some of these notes in </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2021/08/27/can-you-control-the-past/"><span>Can you control the past?</span></a><span>)</span></p><p><span>But I can imagine other framings too.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fno2t3jb6lyii"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo2t3jb6lyii">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There’s shades of logical causation in the three papers </span><a href="https://claude.ai/share/d0bfed5a-7fb2-478f-837f-1c0f7de7b4cb"><span>here</span></a><span>, and shades of updatelessness in Fisher’s and Gauthier’s disposition-based decision theories, Parfit’s “rational irrationality”, McClennen’s “resolute choice” (“it is rational to follow through on plans even when they become locally harmful”), Bratman (Intentions, Plans, and Practical Reason, 1987), and </span><i><span>especially </span></i><a href="https://philpapers.org/archive/MEABAI.pdf?__cf_chl_f_tk=qK5jtWEyGP1Y.x0yfrt5wlI0DRQiTmMwpkCeLiUUA0s-1783106418-1.0.1.1-lfDsq902jxfBGs0E8oQcfVgH9y3KG46xkChi64uKDhI"><span>Meacham’s cohesive decision theory</span></a><span> (“do as you would have bound yourself to do”) (footnote 34 is incredibly fascinating and prescient as an early discussion of the idea of updatelessness, and of how updateless to be).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkcru4q3ydg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkcru4q3ydg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Of decision theory, metaphysics (e.g. Tegmark IV), and computationalism in philosophy of mind.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbze4thpwu79"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbze4thpwu79">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or talk about the very mundane </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w9vQNTSm7dthgathH/reframing-lesswrong-style-decision-theory-as-commitment" rel="noreferrer"><span>commitment theory</span></a><span> instead, my metaphysically minimalist version of LessWrong-style decision theory - your choice. But don’t do this weird in-between thing of arguing for fancy FDT and simultaneously pretending there’s no metaphysics going on. You can’t have your cake and eat it too.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oZzRHiSZPcjrWHeoE/stop-doing-decision-theory-without-metaphysics#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Against the AI framing multiverse: Introducing AI StopWatch](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Rk57ePmRsw4C5PLm2/against-the-ai-framing-multiverse-introducing-ai-stopwatch)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Against the AI framing multiverse: Introducing AI StopWatch</span></p><p><span>In my long years as a classroom teacher, it was my experience that the kid most likely to speak up during discussion was the one who did the reading.</span></p><p><span>I think it’s true for adults, too. I know that’s not exactly revelatory, but it’s one of the guiding principles behind </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/"><span>AI StopWatch</span></a><span>, the experimental newsroom we (parts of the MIRI comms team) launched in May, after a month of closed beta testing.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 66.01208459214502%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1bdc8f251e3e9a67b4aaf079f9087aa30ea544d61ed48599bcfa034a14d83c44/gxhokpdcnp3c1kbywfkm" /></figure><p><span>StopWatch’s other guiding principle is that the reading needs to make sense. Imagine if every page that kid read came from a different book by the same name, and if most of these different books were just reflections of what people who didn’t read it imagined it would be.</span></p><p><span>If the book in question were </span><i><span>To Kill a Mockingbird</span></i><span>, then (speaking from experience) this would mean that on one page, the Finch family is Black and oppressed. The next page is a hunting manual. Flip the page again, and Scout is a boy. Flip to a page near the end, and Atticus might win the case.</span></p><p><span>This is how the media landscape around AI looks to me. Even when the facts agree, the </span><i><span>frames</span></i><span> are so varied that it’s like the articles, op-eds, and videos are drawn from many parallel universes, each reflecting a different story people used to imagine about how AI would play out.</span></p><p><span>On the same day, sometimes in </span><i><span>the same publication</span></i><span>, you will find artifacts from universes where AI is coming for all the jobs, and others from universes where AI is a useless regurgitator — a scam, even.</span></p><p><span>Represented are universes where AI can of course never be catastrophically dangerous because it can only do what people ask. But so are universes where AI will of course be catastrophically dangerous </span><i><span>because</span></i><span> it will do what people ask — and still other universes (rarer) where AI will of course be catastrophically dangerous because it won’t actually do what people ask.</span></p><p><span>In some universes, AI will be safely constrained, because it will never have [special human quality] or be able to experience [quintessentially human thing]. In others, AI will be prone to dangerous excess, because it will never be bound by [special human quality] or be able to experience [quintessentially human thing]. (Universes where AI can, in fact, gain [special human quality] and may have already done so are either very rare or very underrepresented.)</span></p><p><span>Some universes nervously watch for the day when AI will wake up and become sentient, because that’s when it will turn on its creators. But in others, the reverse is true, and the urgent thing is to give AI consciousness so that it can learn to love us before it’s too late. But in many, perhaps most universes, machines can of course never be conscious — that’s a special human quality. (Universes where consciousness may not be a binary condition appear scarce, along with those where consciousness has little bearing on AI’s destructive potential.)</span></p><p><span>The distribution of universes dropping artifacts into our media is not stable or consistent. Since mid-January, I have been plotting the patterns like one might plot the weather. A key finding is that news objects act as conduits that preferentially channel some universes over others.</span></p><p><span>Take a Molotov cocktail, for example: When one hit the gate of Sam Altman’s home on April 10, we saw a modest bump in missives from universes matching the suspect’s concern that AI is an existential risk. These were soon drowned out by transmissions from universes where x-risk concerns are just dangerous fearmongering, and from others where x-risk is a cynical branding strategy used to hype company valuations.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Rk57ePmRsw4C5PLm2/1b1e30ada06bb8a8d4076a4d14137d601db634b4c283e6a7a786b322b1a6c19b/funvt6stuub60ajq9qca" /><p><span>At the end of February, Claude’s reported assistance with the American military’s assault on Iran brought the first big spike in artifacts from universes where AI is the key to battlefield dominance. Such spikes seem to somewhat suppress our contact with universes where human qualities are irreplaceable.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1abf1958b75aa9e701ee30b2be216767ef3fc8f573dc6138408a8f17b3bb3ef0/kif0u95391ju6bcm1zik" /><p><span>Don’t put too much stock in my charts. The methodology behind them is crude: In six months, my colleagues and I have ingested roughly 3,400 media artifacts into my database. For each, I’ve had Claude Opus identify up to four implicit assumptions it makes. Once a week, I have Claude cluster the previous seven days’ framing assumptions according to some stable descriptors and then plot the relative rankings of these clusters. (You can also see their absolute tallies, along with their descriptors, in my </span><a href="https://mitchhowe.github.io/word-clouds/framing-dashboard-2026-07-14-public.html"><span>interactive dashboard</span></a><span>, a vibe-coded tool I haven’t tried to fully de-jank.)</span></p><p><span>My point is that the frames around AI are all over the place. In this media environment, I don’t know how anyone without long exposure to AI insiders is supposed to form a useful model of AI’s shape and trajectory. I think that’s a problem. It’s a main reason we started </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/"><span>AI StopWatch</span></a><span> — a Substack for helping non-insiders keep up with AI so they feel more confident speaking up about the dangers of racing to superintelligence. I wanted a news site I could easily recommend to my mother, my congressional representative, content creators, journalists who aren’t already plugged into insider chatter, and everyone in between.</span></p><p><span>There are plenty of AI news aggregators out there, but none that matched my requirements. The automated sites don’t address the chaotic framing problem. Others are a jargony firehose (love you, </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/"><span>Zvi</span></a><span>!), or are insufficiently discriminating about their sources. Some have a stable frame, but that frame doesn’t reflect the universe I think we actually live in. Some only publish sporadically.</span></p><p><span>At AI StopWatch, we typically post between two and five times a day, seven days a week. Every evening, we compile the day’s posts — which are low on jargon, high on water cooler discussability (we hope) — into an email-friendly “Daily Digest,” which we also release as a ~10-20 minute audio podcast a few hours later. We think the podcast is a stand-out feature; a surprising number of people we know in our target audience can’t bring themselves to regularly read written news or blogs but already subscribe to many podcasts.</span></p><p><span>Our posts cover a timely mix of stories we </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/i/202780795/did-china-obtain-a-key-ai-chip-making-machine"><span>think</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/p/so-that-those-who-live-after-may#%C2%A7ten-places-where-pope-leo-xivs-new-encyclical-matters-for-ai"><span>might</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/p/where-ais-go-to-think"><span>matter</span></a><span> and stories that make </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/i/194577705/gimmicky-step-makes-investors-leap"><span>good</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/p/no-choice-your-voice-can-take-me"><span>conversation</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/p/whats-your-price"><span>starters</span></a><span>.
